People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess… I’m here because I’ve been feeling really weighed down by this thought that I’ve messed things up for my family. We took on this mortgage, and I keep feeling like that was on me, like I pushed us into something too big, and now every little thing feels like proof that I’m not handling life the way I’m supposed to.

Like at work, I’m a waiter, and if I forget something small or a customer looks annoyed, I don’t just think, “Okay, that was a mistake.” I go straight to, “Of course, you’re useless, you can’t even do this right.” And then it sort of spirals into feeling like I deserve whatever bad thing happens because I brought it on myself. I know that probably sounds dramatic, but it feels very real in the moment, and it’s been hard to shake.

I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I’ve definitely had rough times before, like anyone has, where I was down for a while or felt stressed or disappointed with myself. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’m here because I’ve been feeling really weighed down by this thought that I’ve messed things up for my family. We took on this mortgage, and I keep feeling like that was on me, like I pushed us into something too big, and now every little thing feels like proof that I’m not handling life the way I’m supposed to.\n\nLike at work, I’m a waiter, and if I forget something small or a customer looks annoyed, I don’t just think, “Okay, that was a mistake.” I go straight to, “Of course, you’re useless, you can’t even do this right.” And then it sort of spirals into feeling like I deserve whatever bad thing happens because I brought it on myself. I know that probably sounds dramatic, but it feels very real in the moment, and it’s been hard to shake.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel nervous or on edge, even when there's no obvious reason to feel that way?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I do get tense sometimes, but I usually connect it to something—like work, money, the house, whether I’ve disappointed someone. I don’t know if I’d say it comes out of nowhere for no reason.\n\nIt’s more like my mind starts chewing on something and then my body follows along, if that makes sense. But just sitting there feeling nervous with no obvious reason… I haven’t really noticed that as its own thing. Maybe I’m not paying attention to it the right way, though.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, I think so. It’s not always like I’m openly crying or anything obvious like that. It’s more this heavy, empty feeling in my chest that just kind of sits there.\n\nI’ll be at work carrying plates, smiling at customers, saying the usual things because that’s what you’re supposed to do, and from the outside I probably look normal enough. But inside it feels like I’m just moving through the day without really feeling anything good. Like I’m performing being okay, if that makes sense. Some moments are lighter than others, but lately that sadness or emptiness is there a lot of the time.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy, empty feeling inside?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a while. If I try to put an actual time on it, I’d say about a year and a couple months now. Maybe around fourteen months.\n\nIt hasn’t been exactly the same every single day, like some days I can distract myself or I get through work and it’s not as loud, but the depressed feeling and the guilt have been around for that long overall. It’s not like it just started last week or even a few months ago. It’s been kind of sitting there in the background for more than a year.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods over the last 14 months where you felt completely back to normal, without any of these depressed feelings or guilt?\n\nPatient: No… not completely back to normal, I don’t think. There have been better stretches, like days where I’m more distracted or work keeps me busy enough that I’m not sitting with it the whole time. And sometimes I can laugh at something or have a decent few hours, so it’s not like every single minute has been the exact same.\n\nBut if you mean, like, a real period where the depressed feeling and the guilt were just gone and I felt like myself again… no, I can’t really say that happened. Over this whole roughly year and two months, it’s been there in some form. Maybe quieter sometimes, more in the background, but not absent.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any other periods like this in your life before the last fourteen months?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I’ve definitely had rough times before, like anyone has, where I was down for a while or felt stressed or disappointed with myself. But if you mean something like this same kind of ongoing heaviness that just keeps hanging around day after day for months and months… I don’t know that I can point to another time like that clearly.\n\nIt’s hard because when I look back, my memory kind of blurs things together. I can remember being unhappy during certain periods, but I don’t know if it was this persistent, or if it lifted more normally back then. This last year or so feels different mostly because of how long it’s been sitting there without really going away.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep over the last fourteen months?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I sleep, but I haven’t been keeping track of it or anything, so it’s hard for me to say whether it’s changed in a clear way over the whole fourteen months.\n\nSome nights feel normal enough, and other nights I might stay up too late just because I’m thinking or I don’t feel ready to go to bed, but I don’t know if that’s really a sleep problem or just… life, you know? ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if I’d say there’s been a clear decrease, exactly. I mean… I still enjoy some things when I actually do them. Like if I’m watching something funny, or having a decent conversation, or eating something I like, I can still feel that it’s good. It’s not like everything went completely flat or nothing ever feels nice.

But I guess sometimes I don’t go looking for enjoyable things as much, if that makes sense. Like I might not make plans or I might put things off, but I don’t know if that’s because I’m enjoying them less or just because I’m tired of thinking about everything. So… I’m not totally sure. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed a big obvious loss of pleasure, though.

Maybe I’m not paying attention to it the right way, though.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, I think so. It’s not always like I’m openly crying or anything obvious like that. It’s more this heavy, empty feeling in my chest that just kind of sits there.\n\nI’ll be at work carrying plates, smiling at customers, saying the usual things because that’s what you’re supposed to do, and from the outside I probably look normal enough. But inside it feels like I’m just moving through the day without really feeling anything good. Like I’m performing being okay, if that makes sense. Some moments are lighter than others, but lately that sadness or emptiness is there a lot of the time.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy, empty feeling inside?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a while. If I try to put an actual time on it, I’d say about a year and a couple months now. Maybe around fourteen months.\n\nIt hasn’t been exactly the same every single day, like some days I can distract myself or I get through work and it’s not as loud, but the depressed feeling and the guilt have been around for that long overall. It’s not like it just started last week or even a few months ago. It’s been kind of sitting there in the background for more than a year.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods over the last 14 months where you felt completely back to normal, without any of these depressed feelings or guilt?\n\nPatient: No… not completely back to normal, I don’t think. There have been better stretches, like days where I’m more distracted or work keeps me busy enough that I’m not sitting with it the whole time. And sometimes I can laugh at something or have a decent few hours, so it’s not like every single minute has been the exact same.\n\nBut if you mean, like, a real period where the depressed feeling and the guilt were just gone and I felt like myself again… no, I can’t really say that happened. Over this whole roughly year and two months, it’s been there in some form. Maybe quieter sometimes, more in the background, but not absent.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any other periods like this in your life before the last fourteen months?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I’ve definitely had rough times before, like anyone has, where I was down for a while or felt stressed or disappointed with myself. But if you mean something like this same kind of ongoing heaviness that just keeps hanging around day after day for months and months… I don’t know that I can point to another time like that clearly.\n\nIt’s hard because when I look back, my memory kind of blurs things together. I can remember being unhappy during certain periods, but I don’t know if it was this persistent, or if it lifted more normally back then. This last year or so feels different mostly because of how long it’s been sitting there without really going away.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep over the last fourteen months?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I sleep, but I haven’t been keeping track of it or anything, so it’s hard for me to say whether it’s changed in a clear way over the whole fourteen months.\n\nSome nights feel normal enough, and other nights I might stay up too late just because I’m thinking or I don’t feel ready to go to bed, but I don’t know if that’s really a sleep problem or just… life, you know? I haven’t noticed one big pattern that I could point to, like “this started then” or “it’s been this many hours every night.” I guess I’d say I’m not sure I’ve noticed a definite change.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any decrease in the pleasure you get from activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say there’s been a clear decrease, exactly. I mean… I still enjoy some things when I actually do them. Like if I’m watching something funny, or having a decent conversation, or eating something I like, I can still feel that it’s good. It’s not like everything went completely flat or nothing ever feels nice.\n\nBut I guess sometimes I don’t go looking for enjoyable things as much, if that makes sense. It’s more like… most of the day, most days, I think. Not every single second, because if I’m busy at work or someone’s talking to me, I can sort of get pulled into what I’m doing. But underneath it, there’s this heaviness that’s just kind of there.

It’s hard to describe without sounding dramatic, but it feels like this heavy, empty feeling in my chest. Like I’m going through the motions a lot of the time. At the restaurant, I’ll be carrying plates, smiling at people, saying the usual things, trying to be polite and normal because that’s what I’m supposed to do. And from the outside I probably look fine enough. But inside it doesn’t feel connected to anything good. It’s like I’m acting the part of myself.

So I guess it can fade into the background for a bit, but I wouldn’t call it just coming and going. It’s more like it’s sitting there most of the day, and I notice it whenever things get quiet or I’m not distracted.

We took on this mortgage, and I keep feeling like that was on me, like I pushed us into something too big, and now every little thing feels like proof that I’m not handling life the way I’m supposed to.\n\nLike at work, I’m a waiter, and if I forget something small or a customer looks annoyed, I don’t just think, “Okay, that was a mistake.” I go straight to, “Of course, you’re useless, you can’t even do this right.” And then it sort of spirals into feeling like I deserve whatever bad thing happens because I brought it on myself. I know that probably sounds dramatic, but it feels very real in the moment, and it’s been hard to shake.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel nervous or on edge, even when there's no obvious reason to feel that way?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I do get tense sometimes, but I usually connect it to something—like work, money, the house, whether I’ve disappointed someone. I don’t know if I’d say it comes out of nowhere for no reason.\n\nIt’s more like my mind starts chewing on something and then my body follows along, if that makes sense. But just sitting there feeling nervous with no obvious reason… I haven’t really noticed that as its own thing. Maybe I’m not paying attention to it the right way, though.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, I think so. It’s not always like I’m openly crying or anything obvious like that. It’s more this heavy, empty feeling in my chest that just kind of sits there.\n\nI’ll be at work carrying plates, smiling at customers, saying the usual things because that’s what you’re supposed to do, and from the outside I probably look normal enough. But inside it feels like I’m just moving through the day without really feeling anything good. Like I’m performing being okay, if that makes sense. Some moments are lighter than others, but lately that sadness or emptiness is there a lot of the time.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy, empty feeling inside?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a while. If I try to put an actual time on it, I’d say about a year and a couple months now. Maybe around fourteen months.\n\nIt hasn’t been exactly the same every single day, like some days I can distract myself or I get through work and it’s not as loud, but the depressed feeling and the guilt have been around for that long overall. It’s not like it just started last week or even a few months ago. It’s been kind of sitting there in the background for more than a year.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods over the last 14 months where you felt completely back to normal, without any of these depressed feelings or guilt?\n\nPatient: No… not completely back to normal, I don’t think. There have been better stretches, like days where I’m more distracted or work keeps me busy enough that I’m not sitting with it the whole time. And sometimes I can laugh at something or have a decent few hours, so it’s not like every single minute has been the exact same.\n\nBut if you mean, like, a real period where the depressed feeling and the guilt were just gone and I felt like myself again… no, I can’t really say that happened. Over this whole roughly year and two months, it’s been there in some form. Maybe quieter sometimes, more in the background, but not absent.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any other periods like this in your life before the last fourteen months?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I’ve definitely had rough times before, like anyone has, where I was down for a while or felt stressed or disappointed with myself. But if you mean something like this same kind of ongoing heaviness that just keeps hanging around day after day for months and months… I don’t know that I can point to another time like that clearly.\n\nIt’s hard because when I look back, my memory kind of blurs things together. I can remember being unhappy during certain periods, but I don’t know if it was this persistent, or if it lifted more normally back then. This last year or so feels different mostly because of how long it’s been sitting there without really going away.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep over the last fourteen months?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I sleep, but I haven’t been keeping track of it or anything, so it’s hard for me to say whether it’s changed in a clear way over the whole fourteen months.\n\nSome nights feel normal enough, and other nights I might stay up too late just because I’m thinking or I don’t feel ready to go to bed, but I don’t know if that’s really a sleep problem or just… life, you know? I haven’t noticed one big pattern that I could point to, like “this started then” or “it’s been this many hours every night.” I guess I’d say I’m not sure I’ve noticed a definite change.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any decrease in the pleasure you get from activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say there’s been a clear decrease, exactly. I mean… I still enjoy some things when I actually do them. Like if I’m watching something funny, or having a decent conversation, or eating something I like, I can still feel that it’s good. It’s not like everything went completely flat or nothing ever feels nice.\n\nBut I guess sometimes I don’t go looking for enjoyable things as much, if that makes sense. Like I might not make plans or I might put things off, but I don’t know if that’s because I’m enjoying them less or just because I’m tired of thinking about everything. So… I’m not totally sure. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed a big obvious loss of pleasure, though.\n\nDoctor: Are these feelings of sadness and heaviness something you experience for most of the day, or are they more intermittent?\n\nPatient: It’s more like… most of the day, most days, I think. Not every single second, because if I’m busy at work or someone’s talking to me, I can sort of get pulled into what I’m doing. But underneath it, there’s this heaviness that’s just kind of there.\n\nIt’s hard to describe without sounding dramatic, but it feels like this heavy, empty feeling in my chest. Like I’m going through the motions a lot of the time. At the restaurant, I’ll be carrying plates, smiling at people, saying the usual things, trying to be polite and normal because that’s what I’m supposed to do. And from the outside I probably look fine enough. But inside it doesn’t feel connected to anything good. It’s like I’m acting the part of myself.\n\nSo I guess it can fade into the background for a bit, but I wouldn’t call it just coming and going. It’s more like it’s sitting there most of the day, and I notice it whenever things get quiet or I’m not distracted.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think it really changes with the seasons, not in a way I’ve noticed. Like, I can’t say winter makes it this way or summer fixes it or anything like that.

It’s more just… most days, regardless of what it’s like outside, there’s this heavy, empty feeling in my chest. I’ll be at work carrying plates, smiling at people, saying “sure, no problem,” doing all the normal waiter stuff because that’s what I’m supposed to do. And I can do it, I guess. But inside it doesn’t feel like I’m actually feeling anything good from it. It’s more like I’m going through the motions and trying to look normal.

So, no, I wouldn’t really connect it to the seasons. It feels more constant than that.

But underneath it, there’s this heaviness that’s just kind of there.\n\nIt’s hard to describe without sounding dramatic, but it feels like this heavy, empty feeling in my chest. Like I’m going through the motions a lot of the time. At the restaurant, I’ll be carrying plates, smiling at people, saying the usual things, trying to be polite and normal because that’s what I’m supposed to do. And from the outside I probably look fine enough. But inside it doesn’t feel connected to anything good. It’s like I’m acting the part of myself.\n\nSo I guess it can fade into the background for a bit, but I wouldn’t call it just coming and going. It’s more like it’s sitting there most of the day, and I notice it whenever things get quiet or I’m not distracted.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your feelings of sadness and heaviness change with the seasons?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it really changes with the seasons, not in a way I’ve noticed. Like, I can’t say winter makes it this way or summer fixes it or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more just… most days, regardless of what it’s like outside, there’s this heavy, empty feeling in my chest. I’ll be at work carrying plates, smiling at people, saying “sure, no problem,” doing all the normal waiter stuff because that’s what I’m supposed to do. And I can do it, I guess. But inside it doesn’t feel like I’m actually feeling anything good from it. It’s more like I’m going through the motions and trying to look normal.\n\nSo, no, I wouldn’t really connect it to the seasons. I’m not sure I’d put it that strongly, like “no longer enjoy.” I don’t think it’s completely gone. If I actually sit down and do something I used to like, sometimes I can still get something out of it. It might take me a while to get into it, or I might not feel motivated to start, but once I’m there, it’s not like I feel absolutely nothing.

I guess that’s where I get a little confused with the question, because I don’t always seek things out the way I used to, but I don’t know if that means I don’t enjoy them anymore. It’s more like everything feels like effort first, and the enjoyment part is less obvious until I’m already doing it. So… no, I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed that I no longer enjoy pleasurable things. It’s not that clear-cut for me.

But inside it feels like I’m just moving through the day without really feeling anything good. Like I’m performing being okay, if that makes sense. Some moments are lighter than others, but lately that sadness or emptiness is there a lot of the time.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy, empty feeling inside?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a while. If I try to put an actual time on it, I’d say about a year and a couple months now. Maybe around fourteen months.\n\nIt hasn’t been exactly the same every single day, like some days I can distract myself or I get through work and it’s not as loud, but the depressed feeling and the guilt have been around for that long overall. It’s not like it just started last week or even a few months ago. It’s been kind of sitting there in the background for more than a year.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods over the last 14 months where you felt completely back to normal, without any of these depressed feelings or guilt?\n\nPatient: No… not completely back to normal, I don’t think. There have been better stretches, like days where I’m more distracted or work keeps me busy enough that I’m not sitting with it the whole time. And sometimes I can laugh at something or have a decent few hours, so it’s not like every single minute has been the exact same.\n\nBut if you mean, like, a real period where the depressed feeling and the guilt were just gone and I felt like myself again… no, I can’t really say that happened. Over this whole roughly year and two months, it’s been there in some form. Maybe quieter sometimes, more in the background, but not absent.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any other periods like this in your life before the last fourteen months?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I’ve definitely had rough times before, like anyone has, where I was down for a while or felt stressed or disappointed with myself. But if you mean something like this same kind of ongoing heaviness that just keeps hanging around day after day for months and months… I don’t know that I can point to another time like that clearly.\n\nIt’s hard because when I look back, my memory kind of blurs things together. I can remember being unhappy during certain periods, but I don’t know if it was this persistent, or if it lifted more normally back then. This last year or so feels different mostly because of how long it’s been sitting there without really going away.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep over the last fourteen months?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I sleep, but I haven’t been keeping track of it or anything, so it’s hard for me to say whether it’s changed in a clear way over the whole fourteen months.\n\nSome nights feel normal enough, and other nights I might stay up too late just because I’m thinking or I don’t feel ready to go to bed, but I don’t know if that’s really a sleep problem or just… life, you know? I haven’t noticed one big pattern that I could point to, like “this started then” or “it’s been this many hours every night.” I guess I’d say I’m not sure I’ve noticed a definite change.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any decrease in the pleasure you get from activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say there’s been a clear decrease, exactly. I mean… I still enjoy some things when I actually do them. Like if I’m watching something funny, or having a decent conversation, or eating something I like, I can still feel that it’s good. It’s not like everything went completely flat or nothing ever feels nice.\n\nBut I guess sometimes I don’t go looking for enjoyable things as much, if that makes sense. Like I might not make plans or I might put things off, but I don’t know if that’s because I’m enjoying them less or just because I’m tired of thinking about everything. So… I’m not totally sure. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed a big obvious loss of pleasure, though.\n\nDoctor: Are these feelings of sadness and heaviness something you experience for most of the day, or are they more intermittent?\n\nPatient: It’s more like… most of the day, most days, I think. Not every single second, because if I’m busy at work or someone’s talking to me, I can sort of get pulled into what I’m doing. But underneath it, there’s this heaviness that’s just kind of there.\n\nIt’s hard to describe without sounding dramatic, but it feels like this heavy, empty feeling in my chest. Like I’m going through the motions a lot of the time. At the restaurant, I’ll be carrying plates, smiling at people, saying the usual things, trying to be polite and normal because that’s what I’m supposed to do. And from the outside I probably look fine enough. But inside it doesn’t feel connected to anything good. It’s like I’m acting the part of myself.\n\nSo I guess it can fade into the background for a bit, but I wouldn’t call it just coming and going. It’s more like it’s sitting there most of the day, and I notice it whenever things get quiet or I’m not distracted.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your feelings of sadness and heaviness change with the seasons?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it really changes with the seasons, not in a way I’ve noticed. Like, I can’t say winter makes it this way or summer fixes it or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more just… most days, regardless of what it’s like outside, there’s this heavy, empty feeling in my chest. I’ll be at work carrying plates, smiling at people, saying “sure, no problem,” doing all the normal waiter stuff because that’s what I’m supposed to do. And I can do it, I guess. But inside it doesn’t feel like I’m actually feeling anything good from it. It’s more like I’m going through the motions and trying to look normal.\n\nSo, no, I wouldn’t really connect it to the seasons. It feels more constant than that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you no longer enjoy activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that strongly, like “no longer enjoy.” I don’t think it’s completely gone. If I actually sit down and do something I used to like, sometimes I can still get something out of it. It might take me a while to get into it, or I might not feel motivated to start, but once I’m there, it’s not like I feel absolutely nothing.\n\nI guess that’s where I get a little confused with the question, because I don’t always seek things out the way I used to, but I don’t know if that means I don’t enjoy them anymore. It’s more like everything feels like effort first, and the enjoyment part is less obvious until I’m already doing it. So… no, I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed that I no longer enjoy pleasurable things. It’s not that clear-cut for me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

It might take me a while to get into it, or I might not feel motivated to start, but once I’m there, it’s not like I feel absolutely nothing.\n\nI guess that’s where I get a little confused with the question, because I don’t always seek things out the way I used to, but I don’t know if that means I don’t enjoy them anymore. It’s more like everything feels like effort first, and the enjoyment part is less obvious until I’m already doing it. So… no, I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed that I no longer enjoy pleasurable things. It’s not that clear-cut for me.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave your feelings of sadness and heaviness stayed about the same over the last fourteen months, or have they gotten better or worse at any point?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 38 / waiter_or_waitress / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Depressed_Mood\nmanifestation: \"Most days there’s this heavy, empty feeling in my chest, like I’m going through the motions carrying plates and smiling because I’m supposed to, not because I feel anything good.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s been… mostly the same, I think. I mean, there are better hours or worse hours, depending on what I’m doing or how busy I am, but the overall feeling hasn’t really lifted in any lasting way.

Most days there’s still that heavy, empty feeling in my chest. It’s like I’ll be at work carrying plates, smiling at tables, saying the right things because that’s what I’m supposed to do, and I can get through it. I can look normal enough, I guess. But it doesn’t feel like I’m doing it because I feel good or connected to anything. It’s more like I’m just moving through the motions.

So if you mean over time, like whether there was a stretch where it clearly got better or worse, I don’t know that I’d say that. It’s more like it’s been sitting there in the background most days, and sometimes I’m distracted from it, but when things quiet down, it’s still there.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’m here because I’ve been feeling really weighed down by this thought that I’ve messed things up for my family. We took on this mortgage, and I keep feeling like that was on me, like I pushed us into something too big, and now every little thing feels like proof that I’m not handling life the way I’m supposed to.\n\nLike at work, I’m a waiter, and if I forget something small or a customer looks annoyed, I don’t just think, “Okay, that was a mistake.” I go straight to, “Of course, you’re useless, you can’t even do this right.” And then it sort of spirals into feeling like I deserve whatever bad thing happens because I brought it on myself. I know that probably sounds dramatic, but it feels very real in the moment, and it’s been hard to shake.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel nervous or on edge, even when there's no obvious reason to feel that way?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I do get tense sometimes, but I usually connect it to something—like work, money, the house, whether I’ve disappointed someone. I don’t know if I’d say it comes out of nowhere for no reason.\n\nIt’s more like my mind starts chewing on something and then my body follows along, if that makes sense. But just sitting there feeling nervous with no obvious reason… I haven’t really noticed that as its own thing. Maybe I’m not paying attention to it the right way, though.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, I think so. It’s not always like I’m openly crying or anything obvious like that. It’s more this heavy, empty feeling in my chest that just kind of sits there.\n\nI’ll be at work carrying plates, smiling at customers, saying the usual things because that’s what you’re supposed to do, and from the outside I probably look normal enough. But inside it feels like I’m just moving through the day without really feeling anything good. Like I’m performing being okay, if that makes sense. Some moments are lighter than others, but lately that sadness or emptiness is there a lot of the time.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy, empty feeling inside?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a while. If I try to put an actual time on it, I’d say about a year and a couple months now. Maybe around fourteen months.\n\nIt hasn’t been exactly the same every single day, like some days I can distract myself or I get through work and it’s not as loud, but the depressed feeling and the guilt have been around for that long overall. It’s not like it just started last week or even a few months ago. It’s been kind of sitting there in the background for more than a year.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods over the last 14 months where you felt completely back to normal, without any of these depressed feelings or guilt?\n\nPatient: No… not completely back to normal, I don’t think. There have been better stretches, like days where I’m more distracted or work keeps me busy enough that I’m not sitting with it the whole time. And sometimes I can laugh at something or have a decent few hours, so it’s not like every single minute has been the exact same.\n\nBut if you mean, like, a real period where the depressed feeling and the guilt were just gone and I felt like myself again… no, I can’t really say that happened. Over this whole roughly year and two months, it’s been there in some form. Maybe quieter sometimes, more in the background, but not absent.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any other periods like this in your life before the last fourteen months?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I’ve definitely had rough times before, like anyone has, where I was down for a while or felt stressed or disappointed with myself. But if you mean something like this same kind of ongoing heaviness that just keeps hanging around day after day for months and months… I don’t know that I can point to another time like that clearly.\n\nIt’s hard because when I look back, my memory kind of blurs things together. I can remember being unhappy during certain periods, but I don’t know if it was this persistent, or if it lifted more normally back then. This last year or so feels different mostly because of how long it’s been sitting there without really going away.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep over the last fourteen months?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I sleep, but I haven’t been keeping track of it or anything, so it’s hard for me to say whether it’s changed in a clear way over the whole fourteen months.\n\nSome nights feel normal enough, and other nights I might stay up too late just because I’m thinking or I don’t feel ready to go to bed, but I don’t know if that’s really a sleep problem or just… life, you know? I haven’t noticed one big pattern that I could point to, like “this started then” or “it’s been this many hours every night.” I guess I’d say I’m not sure I’ve noticed a definite change.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any decrease in the pleasure you get from activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say there’s been a clear decrease, exactly. I mean… I still enjoy some things when I actually do them. Like if I’m watching something funny, or having a decent conversation, or eating something I like, I can still feel that it’s good. It’s not like everything went completely flat or nothing ever feels nice.\n\nBut I guess sometimes I don’t go looking for enjoyable things as much, if that makes sense. Like I might not make plans or I might put things off, but I don’t know if that’s because I’m enjoying them less or just because I’m tired of thinking about everything. So… I’m not totally sure. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed a big obvious loss of pleasure, though.\n\nDoctor: Are these feelings of sadness and heaviness something you experience for most of the day, or are they more intermittent?\n\nPatient: It’s more like… most of the day, most days, I think. Not every single second, because if I’m busy at work or someone’s talking to me, I can sort of get pulled into what I’m doing. Like I’m going through the motions a lot of the time. At the restaurant, I’ll be carrying plates, smiling at people, saying the usual things, trying to be polite and normal because that’s what I’m supposed to do. And from the outside I probably look fine enough. But inside it doesn’t feel connected to anything good. It’s like I’m acting the part of myself.\n\nSo I guess it can fade into the background for a bit, but I wouldn’t call it just coming and going. It’s more like it’s sitting there most of the day, and I notice it whenever things get quiet or I’m not distracted.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your feelings of sadness and heaviness change with the seasons?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it really changes with the seasons, not in a way I’ve noticed. I’ll be at work carrying plates, smiling at people, saying “sure, no problem,” doing all the normal waiter stuff because that’s what I’m supposed to do. And I can do it, I guess. But inside it doesn’t feel like I’m actually feeling anything good from it. It’s more like I’m going through the motions and trying to look normal.\n\nSo, no, I wouldn’t really connect it to the seasons. delusions_and_hallucinations occur for 2 or more weeks in the absence of a major mood episode (Criterion B)\n  manifestation: There was a stretch of several weeks when my mood was not especially depressed and I was getting through work better, but I still heard the whispering at the condo and still felt watched over the mortgage paperwork.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I’ve had near-missed shifts, mistakes with mortgage paperwork, and more trouble keeping up with POS reports and inventory, which makes me worry I could lose my job and then the condo. I’ve also pulled back from my brother and coworkers enough that people have started noticing I’m not myself.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 8 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about 1 year and 8 months, since after I took on the condo mortgage.\n- key: psychotic_symptoms_duration\n  name: Psychotic Symptoms Duration\n  description: 1 year and 4 months\n  manifestation: The voices and fixed fears about being watched have been happening for about 1 year and 4 months.\n- key: depressive_episode_duration\n  name: Depressive Episode Duration\n  description: 1 year and 2 months\n  manifestation: The depressed, guilty periods have been present for about 1 year and 2 months of this whole time.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 38 / waiter_or_waitress / some_college\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S030\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you find that your feelings of sadness and heaviness change with the seasons?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it really changes with the seasons, not in a way I’ve noticed. Like, I can’t say winter makes it this way or summer fixes it or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more just… most days, regardless of what it’s like outside, there’s this heavy, empty feeling in my chest. I’ll be at work carrying plates, smiling at people, saying “sure, no problem,” doing all the normal waiter stuff because that’s what I’m supposed to do. And I can do it, I guess. But inside it doesn’t feel like I’m actually feeling anything good from it. It’s more like I’m going through the motions and trying to look normal.\n\nSo, no, I wouldn’t really connect it to the seasons. It might take me a while to get into it, or I might not feel motivated to start, but once I’m there, it’s not like I feel absolutely nothing.\n\nI guess that’s where I get a little confused with the question, because I don’t always seek things out the way I used to, but I don’t know if that means I don’t enjoy them anymore. It’s more like everything feels like effort first, and the enjoyment part is less obvious until I’m already doing it. So… no, I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed that I no longer enjoy pleasurable things. It’s not that clear-cut for me.\n\nDoctor: Have your feelings of sadness and heaviness stayed about the same over the last fourteen months, or have they gotten better or worse at any point?\n\nPatient: It’s been… mostly the same, I think. It’s more like I’m just moving through the motions.\n\nSo if you mean over time, like whether there was a stretch where it clearly got better or worse, I don’t know that I’d say that. It’s more like it’s been sitting there in the background most days, and sometimes I’m distracted from it, but when things quiet down, it’s still there.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed any changes in your appetite over the last fourteen months?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… my appetite’s been kind of inconsistent, I guess. Not like one simple direction, like “I stopped eating” or “I’m eating more all the time.” It’s more all over the place.

Some weeks at work, they’ll put out staff meal and I’ll barely touch it. I’ll look at it and know I should eat because I’ve been on my feet, but it just feels like too much effort or I don’t really want anything. Then other nights I get home and it’s the opposite — I’ll just stand in the kitchen and eat whatever’s there, not even because I’m hungry exactly, more like I’m trying to feel settled or filled up somehow.

So yeah, it’s changed in that way. It hasn’t been steady. It sort of swings back and forth.

I know that probably sounds dramatic, but it feels very real in the moment, and it’s been hard to shake.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel nervous or on edge, even when there's no obvious reason to feel that way?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I do get tense sometimes, but I usually connect it to something—like work, money, the house, whether I’ve disappointed someone. I don’t know if I’d say it comes out of nowhere for no reason.\n\nIt’s more like my mind starts chewing on something and then my body follows along, if that makes sense. But just sitting there feeling nervous with no obvious reason… I haven’t really noticed that as its own thing. Maybe I’m not paying attention to it the right way, though.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, I think so. It’s not always like I’m openly crying or anything obvious like that. It’s more this heavy, empty feeling in my chest that just kind of sits there.\n\nI’ll be at work carrying plates, smiling at customers, saying the usual things because that’s what you’re supposed to do, and from the outside I probably look normal enough. Some moments are lighter than others, but lately that sadness or emptiness is there a lot of the time.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy, empty feeling inside?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a while. If I try to put an actual time on it, I’d say about a year and a couple months now. Maybe around fourteen months.\n\nIt hasn’t been exactly the same every single day, like some days I can distract myself or I get through work and it’s not as loud, but the depressed feeling and the guilt have been around for that long overall. It’s not like it just started last week or even a few months ago. It’s been kind of sitting there in the background for more than a year.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods over the last 14 months where you felt completely back to normal, without any of these depressed feelings or guilt?\n\nPatient: No… not completely back to normal, I don’t think. There have been better stretches, like days where I’m more distracted or work keeps me busy enough that I’m not sitting with it the whole time. And sometimes I can laugh at something or have a decent few hours, so it’s not like every single minute has been the exact same.\n\nBut if you mean, like, a real period where the depressed feeling and the guilt were just gone and I felt like myself again… no, I can’t really say that happened. Over this whole roughly year and two months, it’s been there in some form. Maybe quieter sometimes, more in the background, but not absent.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any other periods like this in your life before the last fourteen months?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I’ve definitely had rough times before, like anyone has, where I was down for a while or felt stressed or disappointed with myself. But if you mean something like this same kind of ongoing heaviness that just keeps hanging around day after day for months and months… I don’t know that I can point to another time like that clearly.\n\nIt’s hard because when I look back, my memory kind of blurs things together. I can remember being unhappy during certain periods, but I don’t know if it was this persistent, or if it lifted more normally back then. This last year or so feels different mostly because of how long it’s been sitting there without really going away.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep over the last fourteen months?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I sleep, but I haven’t been keeping track of it or anything, so it’s hard for me to say whether it’s changed in a clear way over the whole fourteen months.\n\nSome nights feel normal enough, and other nights I might stay up too late just because I’m thinking or I don’t feel ready to go to bed, but I don’t know if that’s really a sleep problem or just… life, you know? I haven’t noticed one big pattern that I could point to, like “this started then” or “it’s been this many hours every night.” I guess I’d say I’m not sure I’ve noticed a definite change.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any decrease in the pleasure you get from activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say there’s been a clear decrease, exactly. I mean… I still enjoy some things when I actually do them. Like if I’m watching something funny, or having a decent conversation, or eating something I like, I can still feel that it’s good. It’s not like everything went completely flat or nothing ever feels nice.\n\nBut I guess sometimes I don’t go looking for enjoyable things as much, if that makes sense. Like I might not make plans or I might put things off, but I don’t know if that’s because I’m enjoying them less or just because I’m tired of thinking about everything. So… I’m not totally sure. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed a big obvious loss of pleasure, though.\n\nDoctor: Are these feelings of sadness and heaviness something you experience for most of the day, or are they more intermittent?\n\nPatient: It’s more like… most of the day, most days, I think. Not every single second, because if I’m busy at work or someone’s talking to me, I can sort of get pulled into what I’m doing. But underneath it, there’s this heaviness that’s just kind of there.\n\nIt’s hard to describe without sounding dramatic, but it feels like this heavy, empty feeling in my chest. Like I’m going through the motions a lot of the time. At the restaurant, I’ll be carrying plates, smiling at people, saying the usual things, trying to be polite and normal because that’s what I’m supposed to do. And from the outside I probably look fine enough. But inside it doesn’t feel connected to anything good. It’s like I’m acting the part of myself.\n\nSo I guess it can fade into the background for a bit, but I wouldn’t call it just coming and going. It’s more like it’s sitting there most of the day, and I notice it whenever things get quiet or I’m not distracted.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your feelings of sadness and heaviness change with the seasons?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it really changes with the seasons, not in a way I’ve noticed. Not every single second, because if I’m busy at work or someone’s talking to me, I can sort of get pulled into what I’m doing. But underneath it, there’s this heaviness that’s just kind of there.\n\nIt’s hard to describe without sounding dramatic, but it feels like this heavy, empty feeling in my chest. Like I’m going through the motions a lot of the time. At the restaurant, I’ll be carrying plates, smiling at people, saying the usual things, trying to be polite and normal because that’s what I’m supposed to do. And from the outside I probably look fine enough. But inside it doesn’t feel connected to anything good. It’s like I’m acting the part of myself.\n\nSo I guess it can fade into the background for a bit, but I wouldn’t call it just coming and going. It’s more like it’s sitting there most of the day, and I notice it whenever things get quiet or I’m not distracted.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your feelings of sadness and heaviness change with the seasons?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it really changes with the seasons, not in a way I’ve noticed. Like, I can’t say winter makes it this way or summer fixes it or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more just… most days, regardless of what it’s like outside, there’s this heavy, empty feeling in my chest. I’ll be at work carrying plates, smiling at people, saying “sure, no problem,” doing all the normal waiter stuff because that’s what I’m supposed to do. And I can do it, I guess. But inside it doesn’t feel like I’m actually feeling anything good from it. It’s more like I’m going through the motions and trying to look normal.\n\nSo, no, I wouldn’t really connect it to the seasons. It feels more constant than that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you no longer enjoy activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that strongly, like “no longer enjoy.” I don’t think it’s completely gone. If I actually sit down and do something I used to like, sometimes I can still get something out of it. It might take me a while to get into it, or I might not feel motivated to start, but once I’m there, it’s not like I feel absolutely nothing.\n\nI guess that’s where I get a little confused with the question, because I don’t always seek things out the way I used to, but I don’t know if that means I don’t enjoy them anymore. It’s more like everything feels like effort first, and the enjoyment part is less obvious until I’m already doing it. So… no, I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed that I no longer enjoy pleasurable things. It’s not that clear-cut for me.\n\nDoctor: Have your feelings of sadness and heaviness stayed about the same over the last fourteen months, or have they gotten better or worse at any point?\n\nPatient: It’s been… mostly the same, I think. No… I don’t think so. Not a full two months.

I’ve had better days, or maybe a week here and there where it wasn’t right in my face all the time, but when I look back on it, the heavy, empty feeling and the guilty kind of thoughts have been pretty much there for about a year and two months. I’ll be at work carrying plates, smiling at people, saying “sure, no problem,” doing all the normal waiter stuff because that’s what I’m supposed to do. And I can do it, I guess. It’s more like I’m going through the motions and trying to look normal.\n\nSo, no, I wouldn’t really connect it to the seasons. It feels more constant than that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you no longer enjoy activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that strongly, like “no longer enjoy.” I don’t think it’s completely gone. If I actually sit down and do something I used to like, sometimes I can still get something out of it. It might take me a while to get into it, or I might not feel motivated to start, but once I’m there, it’s not like I feel absolutely nothing.\n\nI guess that’s where I get a little confused with the question, because I don’t always seek things out the way I used to, but I don’t know if that means I don’t enjoy them anymore. But it doesn’t feel like I’m doing it because I feel good or connected to anything. But it’s still been there through this whole period.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed any changes in your energy level over the last fourteen months?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 38 / waiter_or_waitress / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not totally sure how to answer that one. I don’t know that I’ve noticed a clear change, like I could say, “Oh, my energy dropped in this month,” or “then it came back.” It’s more… day to day, I guess.

With my job, sometimes I’m on my feet for a whole shift and I can still get through it because I have to. I’ll be tired afterward, but that also feels kind of normal for waiting tables, so it’s hard for me to separate what’s just physical tiredness from something else. Some days I feel more drained than others, but I don’t know if I’d call it a big change over the whole fourteen months.

So… I guess I haven’t noticed a really obvious shift in energy, not in the way I noticed the heaviness being there. It’s harder for me to pin down.

---------- turn 15 | doctor:inference | doctor.build_inference_prompt() | model=meta-llama/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct ----------

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F33.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’m here because I’ve been feeling really weighed down by this thought that I’ve messed things up for my family. We took on this mortgage, and I keep feeling like that was on me, like I pushed us into something too big, and now every little thing feels like proof that I’m not handling life the way I’m supposed to.\n\nLike at work, I’m a waiter, and if I forget something small or a customer looks annoyed, I don’t just think, “Okay, that was a mistake.” I go straight to, “Of course, you’re useless, you can’t even do this right.” And then it sort of spirals into feeling like I deserve whatever bad thing happens because I brought it on myself. I know that probably sounds dramatic, but it feels very real in the moment, and it’s been hard to shake.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel nervous or on edge, even when there's no obvious reason to feel that way?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I do get tense sometimes, but I usually connect it to something—like work, money, the house, whether I’ve disappointed someone. I don’t know if I’d say it comes out of nowhere for no reason.\n\nIt’s more like my mind starts chewing on something and then my body follows along, if that makes sense. But just sitting there feeling nervous with no obvious reason… I haven’t really noticed that as its own thing. Maybe I’m not paying attention to it the right way, though.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, I think so. It’s not always like I’m openly crying or anything obvious like that. It’s more this heavy, empty feeling in my chest that just kind of sits there.\n\nI’ll be at work carrying plates, smiling at customers, saying the usual things because that’s what you’re supposed to do, and from the outside I probably look normal enough. But inside it feels like I’m just moving through the day without really feeling anything good. Like I’m performing being okay, if that makes sense. Some moments are lighter than others, but lately that sadness or emptiness is there a lot of the time.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy, empty feeling inside?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a while. If I try to put an actual time on it, I’d say about a year and a couple months now. Maybe around fourteen months.\n\nIt hasn’t been exactly the same every single day, like some days I can distract myself or I get through work and it’s not as loud, but the depressed feeling and the guilt have been around for that long overall. It’s not like it just started last week or even a few months ago. It’s been kind of sitting there in the background for more than a year.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods over the last 14 months where you felt completely back to normal, without any of these depressed feelings or guilt?\n\nPatient: No… not completely back to normal, I don’t think. There have been better stretches, like days where I’m more distracted or work keeps me busy enough that I’m not sitting with it the whole time. And sometimes I can laugh at something or have a decent few hours, so it’s not like every single minute has been the exact same.\n\nBut if you mean, like, a real period where the depressed feeling and the guilt were just gone and I felt like myself again… no, I can’t really say that happened. Over this whole roughly year and two months, it’s been there in some form. Maybe quieter sometimes, more in the background, but not absent.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any other periods like this in your life before the last fourteen months?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I’ve definitely had rough times before, like anyone has, where I was down for a while or felt stressed or disappointed with myself. But if you mean something like this same kind of ongoing heaviness that just keeps hanging around day after day for months and months… I don’t know that I can point to another time like that clearly.\n\nIt’s hard because when I look back, my memory kind of blurs things together. I can remember being unhappy during certain periods, but I don’t know if it was this persistent, or if it lifted more normally back then. This last year or so feels different mostly because of how long it’s been sitting there without really going away.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep over the last fourteen months?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I sleep, but I haven’t been keeping track of it or anything, so it’s hard for me to say whether it’s changed in a clear way over the whole fourteen months.\n\nSome nights feel normal enough, and other nights I might stay up too late just because I’m thinking or I don’t feel ready to go to bed, but I don’t know if that’s really a sleep problem or just… life, you know? I haven’t noticed one big pattern that I could point to, like “this started then” or “it’s been this many hours every night.” I guess I’d say I’m not sure I’ve noticed a definite change.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any decrease in the pleasure you get from activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say there’s been a clear decrease, exactly. I mean… I still enjoy some things when I actually do them. Like if I’m watching something funny, or having a decent conversation, or eating something I like, I can still feel that it’s good. It’s not like everything went completely flat or nothing ever feels nice.\n\nBut I guess sometimes I don’t go looking for enjoyable things as much, if that makes sense. Like I might not make plans or I might put things off, but I don’t know if that’s because I’m enjoying them less or just because I’m tired of thinking about everything. So… I’m not totally sure. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed a big obvious loss of pleasure, though.\n\nDoctor: Are these feelings of sadness and heaviness something you experience for most of the day, or are they more intermittent?\n\nPatient: It’s more like… most of the day, most days, I think. Not every single second, because if I’m busy at work or someone’s talking to me, I can sort of get pulled into what I’m doing. But underneath it, there’s this heaviness that’s just kind of there.\n\nIt’s hard to describe without sounding dramatic, but it feels like this heavy, empty feeling in my chest. Like I’m going through the motions a lot of the time. At the restaurant, I’ll be carrying plates, smiling at people, saying the usual things, trying to be polite and normal because that’s what I’m supposed to do. And from the outside I probably look fine enough. But inside it doesn’t feel connected to anything good. It’s like I’m acting the part of myself.\n\nSo I guess it can fade into the background for a bit, but I wouldn’t call it just coming and going. It’s more like it’s sitting there most of the day, and I notice it whenever things get quiet or I’m not distracted.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your feelings of sadness and heaviness change with the seasons?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it really changes with the seasons, not in a way I’ve noticed. Like, I can’t say winter makes it this way or summer fixes it or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more just… most days, regardless of what it’s like outside, there’s this heavy, empty feeling in my chest. I’ll be at work carrying plates, smiling at people, saying “sure, no problem,” doing all the normal waiter stuff because that’s what I’m supposed to do. And I can do it, I guess. But inside it doesn’t feel like I’m actually feeling anything good from it. It’s more like I’m going through the motions and trying to look normal.\n\nSo, no, I wouldn’t really connect it to the seasons. It feels more constant than that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you no longer enjoy activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that strongly, like “no longer enjoy.” I don’t think it’s completely gone. If I actually sit down and do something I used to like, sometimes I can still get something out of it. It might take me a while to get into it, or I might not feel motivated to start, but once I’m there, it’s not like I feel absolutely nothing.\n\nI guess that’s where I get a little confused with the question, because I don’t always seek things out the way I used to, but I don’t know if that means I don’t enjoy them anymore. It’s more like everything feels like effort first, and the enjoyment part is less obvious until I’m already doing it. So… no, I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed that I no longer enjoy pleasurable things. It’s not that clear-cut for me.\n\nDoctor: Have your feelings of sadness and heaviness stayed about the same over the last fourteen months, or have they gotten better or worse at any point?\n\nPatient: It’s been… mostly the same, I think. I mean, there are better hours or worse hours, depending on what I’m doing or how busy I am, but the overall feeling hasn’t really lifted in any lasting way.\n\nMost days there’s still that heavy, empty feeling in my chest. It’s like I’ll be at work carrying plates, smiling at tables, saying the right things because that’s what I’m supposed to do, and I can get through it. I can look normal enough, I guess. But it doesn’t feel like I’m doing it because I feel good or connected to anything. It’s more like I’m just moving through the motions.\n\nSo if you mean over time, like whether there was a stretch where it clearly got better or worse, I don’t know that I’d say that. It’s more like it’s been sitting there in the background most days, and sometimes I’m distracted from it, but when things quiet down, it’s still there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite over the last fourteen months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… my appetite’s been kind of inconsistent, I guess. Not like one simple direction, like “I stopped eating” or “I’m eating more all the time.” It’s more all over the place.\n\nSome weeks at work, they’ll put out staff meal and I’ll barely touch it. I’ll look at it and know I should eat because I’ve been on my feet, but it just feels like too much effort or I don’t really want anything. Then other nights I get home and it’s the opposite — I’ll just stand in the kitchen and eat whatever’s there, not even because I’m hungry exactly, more like I’m trying to feel settled or filled up somehow.\n\nSo yeah, it’s changed in that way. It hasn’t been steady. It sort of swings back and forth.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two months where you didn't feel this heavy, empty feeling and weren't experiencing these depressive thoughts?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. Not a full two months.\n\nI’ve had better days, or maybe a week here and there where it wasn’t right in my face all the time, but when I look back on it, the heavy, empty feeling and the guilty kind of thoughts have been pretty much there for about a year and two months. Like, they shift around in intensity, and sometimes I’m distracted enough that I can function and not be thinking about it every second, but I wouldn’t say there was a whole stretch of two months where it was just gone.\n\nIf anything, it’s more like I got used to carrying it around, so some days I notice it less. But it’s still been there through this whole period.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your energy level over the last fourteen months?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that one. I don’t know that I’ve noticed a clear change, like I could say, “Oh, my energy dropped in this month,” or “then it came back.” It’s more… day to day, I guess.\n\nWith my job, sometimes I’m on my feet for a whole shift and I can still get through it because I have to. I’ll be tired afterward, but that also feels kind of normal for waiting tables, so it’s hard for me to separate what’s just physical tiredness from something else. Some days I feel more drained than others, but I don’t know if I’d call it a big change over the whole fourteen months.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t noticed a really obvious shift in energy, not in the way I noticed the heaviness being there. It’s harder for me to pin down.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

This is the first time it’s lasted this long, I think. I mean, I’ve felt down before in my life, sure, and I’ve had stretches where I felt guilty or heavy about things, but not like this… not where it just sort of stayed around and became the background of everything.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so, no… not like this.

I’ve had times before where I was sad or felt bad about myself, or where things felt harder than usual, but I wouldn’t call them the same as this. They didn’t stretch on and on in the same way, where it feels like it becomes part of the background every day. Before, it was more like I could connect it to something or it would pass after a while, even if it felt awful at the time.

This one feels different mostly because of how long it’s been hanging around. I keep trying to look back and compare it to other times, but I don’t really remember another episode that had this same weight to it or lasted anywhere close to this long. So I guess my answer is… no, not really. Not another one like this.
